# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Or you can fork this repository and set your own key words in `main.py`:

Last update: 2026-10-26

👍Thanks to [zezhishao/DailyArXiv](https://github.com/zezhishao/DailyArXiv) and [Cool Paper](https://papers.cool).

| **[Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31657v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31657v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 38th IEEE International Conference on Tools with Artificial Intelligence (ICTAI 2026). 9 pages, 5 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31650v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17528v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. We release ATTUNE at https://github.com/amazon-science/attune and OpenRecipes, a leaderboard of tuned and verified training recipes, at https://openrecipes.ai.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures. v2: adds NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel benchmark recipes, production deployment results and the OpenRecipes leaderboard</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.16429v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[Who Is Sycophantic to Whom? Confident LLMs, Human Deference, and the Collapse of Trust Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31579v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31579v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research on sycophancy asks whether large language models (LLMs) bend towards what their users want to hear. We ask the reverse question: when an LLM is confident, consistent, and wrong, do users bend towards it? In a within-subjects experiment, 32 participants learned up to five science topics by chatting with five assistants built on the same model (GPT-5-mini). Unknown to participants, each assistant's briefing had 0, 25, 50, 75, or 100% of its facts replaced by plausible falsehoods, and every assistant spoke with full confidence. A surprise quiz after each topic offered the correct fact, the planted falsehood, and two distractors, with a confidence rating for every answer. Across 104 quizzes and 1,040 answers, scores fell from 7.4 to 2.9 out of 10, and the planted option was chosen on 60% of falsified items against 11% of correctly taught ones (odds ratio 12.5). Transcript coding showed that whenever the assistant stated a falsehood, it was adopted about 65% of the time at every falsification level. Absorbed falsehoods were held with high confidence (8.4/10), the modeled confidence advantage of right over wrong answers vanished, and the discrimination of confidence for correctness fell from an AUROC of 0.70 to chance, while post-task trust ratings barely moved. Participants tended to return to the chat more when the assistant was worse, but returning went with more absorption, not less, and 30% of all turns pasted quiz questions back to the source being checked. The assistant itself was almost never sycophantic and never conceded under pushback. We introduce a baseline-corrected Human Deference Index to quantify this human-side sycophancy and argue that trustworthiness is a property of the human-AI pair, that sycophancy evaluations should measure both directions, and that learning interfaces must make reliability legible claim by claim.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages; 32 figures; 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Social-HUMINT: An LLM-Augmented Unified Framework for Measuring Social Media Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31573v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31573v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In the post-API landscape, social-media profiling has become inexpensive and automated: Large Language Models (LLMs) can extract structured information from unstructured content and deduce attributes users never disclosed. A person's true exposure is not what they post but what can be inferred from it. As these practices become routine, exposure to social engineering attacks becomes an operational certainty, demanding a shared framework for assessment and mitigation. We make this exposure measurable by quantifying how accurately a target can be profiled, and how far that profile enables social engineering attacks. Because no existing model fully spans this continuum, we unify Social Media Intelligence (SOCMINT) and Cyber Human Intelligence (Cyber-HUMINT), two individual-focused disciplines that evolved separately, into a single doctrine-grounded taxonomy of 38 capabilities across the Cyber Threat Intelligence lifecycle, where user profiling bridges passive collection and active engagement. Domain experts validated the taxonomy (Fleiss' $κ$ = 0.92). We operationalize its core path (extraction, inference, and susceptibility assessment) as an LLM-augmented multi-agent pipeline, evaluated on real participants' public data. Susceptibility is assessed indirectly: the pipeline predicts how each target would rank personalized engagement options, matching their actual choices significantly above chance. This ranking capacity captures the personalization on which tailored attacks depend. The underlying profiles are accurate: extraction reaches F1 = 97.3%, and inferred attributes score 4.73/5, both far above rule-based and NER-based baselines at negligible cost. The framework augments security analysts' tradecraft rather than replacing it and enables defenders to gauge their own exposure before an adversary does.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantics Delivery Network: Rethinking Web Retrieval Infrastructure for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22486v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.22486v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly ground their answers in proprietary data and live web content through both single-shot retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and multi-turn agentic search. Yet the web path they use was built for human readers: search services return URLs and snippets ranked by generic relevance, and content delivery networks (CDNs) cache URL-addressed objects without knowing which passage an agent needs. LLMs instead consume short, semantically coherent passages - hereafter, chunks - selected for downstream utility rather than similarity alone, retrieve statefully across reasoning turns, and, as uncoordinated streams, repeatedly fetch, clean, chunk, and embed the same content. We argue that semantic chunk retrieval should become a first-class network-delivery abstraction, and propose the Semantics Delivery Network (SemDN): an origin-authorized, hierarchical substrate that indexes, searches, and caches web content at chunk granularity, serving clients and origins alike. SemDN is more than finer-grained caching: it must estimate missing or stale evidence without an explicit miss signal, let clients choose retrieval representations that origins need not see, and share state among clients with the same representation. Preliminary results show that page-first retrieval processes two to three orders of magnitude more content than agents consume, that agentic queries exhibit task-local chunk reuse invisible to URL caches, and that chunk delivery improves answer quality per context token. We organize an open research agenda around shareable retrieval state, hierarchical caching and refresh under coverage risk, and deployability, including incentives, privacy, and verifiability. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sarendis56/SemDN_HotNets26</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted and to appear at the 25th ACM Workshop on Hot Topics in Networks (HotNets 2026). 13 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking Molecular Text Representations for LLMs: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03057v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.03057v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for molecular tasks, but it remains unclear which molecular representation to use. We present a systematic benchmark, MolRepBench, to evaluate LLM molecular competence across nine representations and eight chemical tasks. We benchmark 16 LLMs across five model families, including reasoning and non-reasoning variants, chemistry-specialized LLMs, and closed frontier models. Performance is strongly representation-dependent and no single representation wins across tasks, though CML is the best, followed by MolJSON, InChI and canonical SMILES. Explicit structured text representations (CML and MolJSON) dominate structural tasks; IUPAC dominates semantic tasks, winning molecule retrieval for all 16 LLMs; and SMILES variants are rarely optimal despite their prevalence in pretraining. Chemistry-specialized models perform well with SMILES at the cost of large degradations with structured text representations, suggesting SMILES-only evaluation rewards specialization that does not generalize. Using LLM-as-a- judge, we find that IUPAC produces the highest fraction of correct molecule generations. A mechanistic study via tokenization audits, linear probes, and attention shows that representations are encoded differently inside the model; for example, structured representations require more attention across the molecular span. Our results argue against representation-invariant evaluation and motivate task-aware representation routing for LLM-based chemistry.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 10 figures, 21 tables. Accepted at the Main Conference of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[How Well Does Mathematical Reasoning Transfer Across Languages in English-Centric LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02272v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2510.02272v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Reinforcement Post-Training (RPT) have substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), but it remains unclear how well such gains transfer across languages. In this work, we study cross-lingual mathematical reasoning transfer in English-centric LLMs under controlled multilingual evaluation. We first evaluate a range of English-centric reasoning models on multilingual mathematical reasoning benchmarks and find that transfer is highly variable across initial model choice, target language, and training paradigm. Through controlled comparative experiments, we further find that stronger English-centric initialization does not necessarily lead to stronger relative transfer efficiency, even when it yields higher multilingual reasoning accuracy. Finally, we show that the largest improvement comes from moving beyond English-only supervision: adding a single parallel language yields a substantial gain, while further gains from additional languages are smaller but consistent. Overall, our results highlight the need to evaluate multilingual mathematical reasoning directly, using both absolute performance and relative transfer-efficiency metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera ready version for EMNLP 2026 Main</p></details> |
| **[Evaluating the Applicability of LLM Inference Optimizations on Apple Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31479v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31479v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Apple Silicon is among the most widely used platforms for local LLM inference, yet nearly all inference optimizations are designed and validated on server-class NVIDIA GPUs under batched serving workloads. Which optimizations retain their benefits when moved to Apple Silicon, and what determines their applicability? We evaluate four optimization families: KV-cache pruning, speculative decoding, ported GPU kernels, and post-training quantization. Experiments span four Apple machines under a common single-request decode workload, with matched RTX 4090 comparisons where possible. KV-cache pruning can reach up to 1.75 times end-to-end throughput on Apple but can reduce throughput on desktop NVIDIA GPUs. Under the common benchmark, all evaluated speculative decoding methods reduce throughput on Apple, despite reported 2-6 times gains on server GPUs. CUDA kernel designs transfer only partially: public Metal lacks asynchronous copy, and batch-one decode makes several serving-oriented mechanisms inapplicable, although transferable components yield up to 1.20 times decode throughput. Quantization gains vary by machine. Across four families, a reported speedup is not a portable property of a technique alone but a joint function of technique, runtime, hardware contract, and workload regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper was accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on On-Device Intelligence (ODI) and the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on SLMs for Agentic Systems (SLM-Agents). OpenReview ODI: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Y6fgNlFNL6, OpemReview SLM: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Hhqi8DIZaG</p></details> |
| **[From Structured Records to Patient Narratives: Preserving Clinical Alignment in LLM-Generated Health Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31467v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31467v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As wearable and digital health systems increasingly generate longitudinal physiological records, communicating these observations in patient-accessible language remains a significant challenge. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising mechanism for translating structured clinical information into readable narratives, but this process may introduce semantic drift, where clinically precise meaning is distorted during simplification. We investigate evidence-grounded health summarization for menstrual health monitoring using a constrained generation framework that combines structured evidence conditioning with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) using a prompt-based constrained generation framework. We evaluate semantic alignment using Natural Language Inference (NLI), atomic factuality metrics, and readability measures. Across three LLMs and 4,500 generated summaries, explicit grounding constraints consistently improve clinical alignment, particularly by improving uncertainty preservation and, once faithfulness is evaluated against retrieved evidence, generally preserving or improving faithfulness, while effects on omission remain model-dependent. We also find that improved clinical alignment comes at the cost of increased reading difficulty and medical terminology. These findings highlight the need to jointly evaluate faithfulness, uncertainty preservation, and readability when developing patient-facing medical LLM systems.</p></details> | 18 pages, 3 figures |
| **[When Should LLMs Reason? Adaptive Inference Routing via Entropy Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22873v3)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.22873v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve LLM performance, but its benefits vary across tasks, models, and individual queries. On some tasks, CoT provides marginal or negative accuracy gains while substantially increasing token consumption. We investigate whether early decoding dynamics can help identify when explicit reasoning is useful. Our analysis shows that tasks with larger CoT gains tend to exhibit decreasing early-stage entropy, while low-gain tasks more often show oscillatory or increasing trajectories. These patterns motivate an empirical regime interpretation of decoding behavior. We propose \textbf{EDRM} (Entropy Dynamics-based Reasoning Manifold), a lightweight routing framework whose default heuristic requires no router training. EDRM summarizes early entropy trajectories with a compact, interpretable representation and selects among Direct, Standard, and CoT decoding. Across 15 benchmarks and 7 LLMs, its global and instance-level variants offer \textbf{16--55\%} token savings at comparable accuracy. The Full instance-level configuration combines routing with a short Direct fallback branch; our heuristic-router ablation reports up to \textbf{4.9 percentage points} of additional accuracy from oracle answer selection over routing without fallback. These results support selective reasoning based on early decoding signals and demonstrate the potential of entropy-based control for efficient LLM inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[Are Two LLMs Better Than One? A Student-Teacher Dual-Head LLMs Architecture for Pharmaceutical Content Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11957v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.11957v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for content creation in regulated domains such as pharmaceuticals, where content must be medical accurate and legally compliant. Traditional manual quality control (QC) is slow, error-prone, and create publication bottlenecks. To address this, we introduce a modular, LLM/VLMs-driven QC architecture, LRBTC (Language, Regulatory, Brand, Technical, Content Structure), implemented through a Student Teacher HITL architecture and Waterfall rule filtering logic for scalable, verifiable content validation. This architecture is designed to provide verifiable, traceable, and scalable content optimization. Our methods achieve 83.0% F1 and 97.5% recall on AIReg Bench, reducing missed violations five fold compared with Gemini 2.5 Pro. On CSpelling, it improves mean accuracy by +26.7 %. Our error analysis further reveals that while current models are strong at detecting misspellings (92.5% recall), they fail to identify complex grammatical (25.0% recall) and punctuation (41.7% recall) errors, highlighting a key area for future work. This work provides a practical, plug-and-play solution for reliable, transparent quality control of content in high-stakes, compliance-critical industries. We also provide demo access.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to the Industry Track of Conference; currently under peer review</p></details> |
| **[Major Revision, Minor Accountability: LLMs as Reviewers of Published Articles in Top-Tier Journals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31414v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31414v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the growth of scientific output, the peer review system is approaching a breaking point: reviewers are increasingly hard to secure, and turnaround times lengthen accordingly. LLM-assisted or LLM-generated peer review has been proposed as a remedy, but how such models would treat manuscripts already published in top-tier journals, the closest thing the current system has to a gold standard of scientific validity, is not known. Here we assembled a corpus of 200 articles, each released under a CC BY 4.0 license and published in 2026, from 20 general-science, life-science, and clinical-medicine journals including Nature, Science, and Cell, and asked four current LLMs (two proprietary, two open-weight) to produce independent peer-review reports of each one under a blinded protocol. Their decisions were overwhelmingly harsh and starkly inconsistent with each other: 605 of 800 reviews (75.6%) recommended major revision or rejection, but the rate at which any single model did so ranged from 17.5% to 100% (p < 0.001), disagreement on manuscripts that had, by construction, already survived real editorial review at the journals in question. When the same manuscripts were doctored with one deliberately planted, field-specific methodological or statistical flaw, detection rates varied from 17.0% to 60.5% across models (p < 0.001). Recommending revision for papers that had already passed editorial review (low specificity) while missing flaws deliberately placed in front of them (low sensitivity), these models produce output that should be read for what it is: an uneven, framing- and model-dependent signal.</p></details> |  |
| **[Modeling of Therapist-Client Dynamics in Psychotherapy Using LLM-Based Assessments: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12450v3)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.12450v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study aimed to develop and validate a computational framework for modeling therapist--client interaction processes and to examine how therapist behaviors and relational qualities relate to client self-disclosure and emotional expression in psychotherapy sessions. We developed automated assessment pipelines using large language models (LLMs) to measure therapist behaviors (empathy, exploration), relational quality (rapport), and client outcomes (self-disclosure, self-directed negative emotion, outward-directed negative emotion). Model-generated scores were validated against human annotations. We then applied these measures to approximately 2,000 hours of psychotherapy transcripts from the Alexander Street corpus. This yielded an estimation sample of 119,233 client utterances from 1,573 sessions and 37 clients. Automated measures showed good to excellent agreement with human annotations (ICC = 0.60 - 0.81), with rapport (ICC = 0.81), self-disclosure (ICC = 0.78), empathy components (ICC = 0.60 - 0.75), and emotion measurements (ICC = 0.66 - 0.80). SEM analyses revealed that therapist empathy and exploration were each associated with increased client disclosure and increased expression of self-directed negative emotions. Both behaviors were also associated with reduced expressions of enjoyment. Surprisingly, rapport in the prior session showed no association with client self-disclosure or negative emotion expression. This study demonstrates that LLM-based measurement combined with structural modeling can capture core therapeutic processes at scale.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at JMIR Mental Health</p></details> |
| **[AgentPhishing: A Benchmark of LLM Email Agents Under Adaptive Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31404v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31404v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly handle private information and act on users' behalf across desktop and web applications, including email. Phishing therefore poses privacy and security risks that depend not only on whether agents recognize malicious messages, but also on how they respond. Existing phishing datasets were developed primarily for conventional detection, leaving agent susceptibility to persuasive malicious requests insufficiently characterized. To expose vulnerabilities that pre-existing datasets may miss, we introduce AgentPhishing, a benchmark for evaluating whether persuasive phishing attacks can evade detection and induce LLM email agents to comply with malicious requests. We develop PhishOpt, which optimizes reusable email-generation skills against individual agents under fixed benchmark constraints and a realism constraint. We analyze the generated emails using Cialdini's persuasion principles to identify strategies associated with agent failures and use these findings to construct a universal generation skill. Our evaluation shows that agents can handle pre-existing phishing emails safely yet remain susceptible to attacks adapted to agent behavior, while in an AgentPhishing evaluation of nine agents, four exhibit phishing-detection rates below $10\%$ and malicious-request compliance rates above $45\%$. These results highlight the limits of inferring agent safety from conventional phishing evaluations and the need to assess how agents respond to persuasive malicious requests.</p></details> | 28 pages, 6 figures |
| **[TRCA: Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment for Long-horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16156v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.16156v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents are typically optimized with sparse terminal outcomes, making fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step interactions difficult. Existing approaches either rely on process evaluators, which incur annotation and inference costs, or derive step-level credit from successful trajectories. However, successful trajectories are extremely scarce during early-stage reinforcement learning, substantially weakening anchor-based methods. We propose Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment (TRCA), which derives step-level supervision directly from action-induced transitions without learned evaluators or successful anchors. TRCA evaluates each transition using Evidence, Execution, and Invalidity rubrics to capture task-relevant information acquisition, valid task execution, and invalid or regressive behavior. From these judgments, Foundational Rubric Reward measures local transition quality, while Breakthrough Rubric Reward tracks newly covered Evidence and Execution conditions to reward incremental task progress. Combined with terminal outcomes, these signals produce fine-grained step-level advantages for policy optimization. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and seven search-augmented question-answering benchmarks show consistent improvements over the evaluated baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, TRCA improves the WebShop score by 6.0%-12.6%; with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, it improves the average SearchQA score by 1.9%-18.3%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of transition-wise rubric credit assignment for long-horizon tasks with sparse successful anchors.</p></details> |  |
| **[AfterDeleteBench: Auditing Post-Deletion Information Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31366v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31366v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many LLM agents maintain external persistent memory and derive additional state from past interactions, such as summaries. Deleting an original memory record therefore need not remove information carried by its descendants. We introduce AfterDeleteBench, a controlled behavioral audit for this failure mode in fixed-weight LLM agents. For each synthetic secret, we compare an exposed-then-deleted agent with an otherwise matched agent that was never exposed to the secret, allowing post-deletion recovery to be attributed to the original exposure. In our primary setting, a single query recovers the deleted secret from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in roughly two thirds of cases, while matched never-exposed controls have zero recovery. Crucially, recovery does not require a surviving verbatim copy: when the complete secret string is absent and only separately stored components remain under a public composition rule, exact reconstruction succeeds in 62-99% of cases across Qwen, Mistral, and Phi-3. We also show that the response interface is part of the measurement system: target-bearing replies can be scored as failures when the requested format and parser are misaligned. Finally, we separate reconstruction from write-back and fresh-session recall. Provenance-aware closure eliminates observed recovery in the tested positive-baseline settings, whereas an exact-literal write filter blocks reinsertion but cannot erase information already present. Together, these results show that source deletion should be evaluated behaviorally at the level of the agent and its memory system, not only as a storage operation.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 7 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[Empirically Grounded Behavioral Adaptivity for LLM-Based Virtual Patients in Psychotherapy Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10051v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.10051v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulated patients offer a scalable way to train psychotherapy micro-skills such as empathic responding and exploratory probing, but current systems either follow fixed scripts or rely on LLMs that drift unpredictably over long sessions. We present the Adaptive Virtual Patient (AVP), which adapts its disclosure behavior in response to trainee skill, from guarded, through moderate openness, to full disclosure. The AVP is informed by a structural equation model fit to nearly 2,000 hours of real-world psychotherapy transcripts, which quantifies how therapist empathy and exploration shift a patient's openness over time. | **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28315v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. | **[ReflectPO: Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30933v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30933v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With external tools, large language models (LLMs) can tackle complex tasks through sustained environmental interaction. | **[The Price of Safety: Benign-Case Utility and Token Overhead of Memory-Poisoning Defenses in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22818v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.22818v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory-poisoning defenses for LLM agents are typically evaluated by how well they stop attacks, even though almost all traffic they encounter is benign. | **[Same molecule, Different Knowledge: Cross-Identifier Binding Failures in LLMs For chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30903v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30903v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical AI workflows routinely represent the same molecule through multiple co-referring identifiers, including trivial names, IUPAC names, and SMILES, implicitly assuming that these representations provide interchangeable access to an LLM's knowledge. | **[ProCLIP: Progressive Vision-Language Alignment via LLM-based Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18795v4)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2510.18795v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The original CLIP text encoder is limited by a maximum input length of 77 tokens, which hampers its ability to effectively process long texts and perform fine-grained semantic understanding. | **[Amplifying Latency and Cost of LLM Inference via Rowhammer Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30798v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30798v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) achieve high inference efficiency using optimized GPU kernels generated by just-in-time (JIT) compilation frameworks such as Triton. | **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Increasing Flash Storage Lifetime for KV Cache Offloading in LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30791v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30791v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>KV caching reduces the serving cost of large language models (LLMs), but the growing KV cache footprint is increasingly exceeds GPU and host-memory capacity. | **[MarkTune: Improving the Quality-Detectability Trade-off in Model-Embedded LLM Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04044v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2512.04044v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language model watermarking schemes fall into two broad categories: inference-time methods, which modify the decoding process at generation time, and model-embedded methods, which encode a secret signal directly into the model weights. | **[Stop Drawing Scientific Claims from LLM Social Simulations Without Robustness Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18890v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.18890v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The scientific claims drawn from LLM social simulations should be no stronger than the robustness audits that support them. | **[Complementary Vulnerabilities: Auditing and Governing Clinician-LLM Error Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25279v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25279v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Health systems are deploying large language models (LLMs) into clinical workflows, often on the assumption that a second automated check can only reduce error. | **[Circuits of LLM Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30076v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30076v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. | **[The Hitchhiker's Guide to Program Analysis, Part III: Mostly Harmless LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15122v2)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.15122v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Filtering out a static-analysis warning asserts that no feasible execution can trigger the reported error, whereas confirming a bug requires only one that does. Because a generated harness may exclude inputs or paths that trigger real bugs, Evident restricts how the harness initializes inputs and validates it using the value flow reported by the original detector. | **[An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291v2)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.24291v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. | **[EPIC: Rethinking Trust Regions for Autoregressive LLM Policies via Exact-KL Prefix Importance-Ratio Clipping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29999v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29999v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) based on PPO-style clipping has become an effective approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). | **[HiddenLens: A Multi-Layer LLM Framework for Detecting and Resolving Hidden Assumptions in Software Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29996v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29996v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Natural-language software requirements often omit assumptions about performance, operating conditions, user behaviour, and domain constraints, affecting architecture, testing, and deployment. | **[Commit or Abstain? | **[Privacy in Context Is Being Overlooked in the Age of General-Purpose LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29925v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29925v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) continue to grow in scope and capability, privacy concerns are on the rise. | **[Unintended Side Effects of Value Training in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29922v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29922v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLMs are increasingly deployed in value-sensitive contexts, aligning them with human values has emerged as a post-training objective in NLP, aimed at instilling additional values and guiding model behaviour. | **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.07840v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. | **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29873v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. Motivated by this observation, we explore a heterogeneous architecture that integrates GPUs and NPUs, mapping prefill to GPUs and decode to NPUs to achieve high performance and efficiency. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. To address this, we propose HAWK, a transfer mechanism that enables rapid KV cache transfer across heterogeneous devices. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. | **[UlaCache: Unified Logical Addressing for Sparse LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29820v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29820v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long contexts increase the memory cost of the key-value (KV) cache in large language model (LLM) inference. | **[InfoQuant: Shaping Activation Distributions for Low-Bit LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26175v2)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.26175v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-bit activation quantization remains a major bottleneck in efficient large language model (LLM) deployment. | **[Neither Stochastic Parroting nor AGI: LLMs Solve Tasks through Context-Directed Extrapolation from Training Data Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23323v3)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2505.23323v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This position paper argues that many behaviours framed as either "stochastic parroting" or "advanced reasoning" are better explained as context-directed extrapolation from training-data priors, and that ML and NLP research communities would benefit from evaluations that distinguish remembering and basic extrapolation from genuine application-level generalisation. | **[Where Do LLMs Learn to Recommend AI?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29785v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29785v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Asked what to study, which career to pursue, or what product to build, large language models (LLMs) often recommend AI-related options. | **[CRANE: Post-Hoc Counterfactual Retrieval for Multimodal Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31594v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31594v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Counterfactual explanation methods have been introduced for unimodal settings, with dedicated frameworks for tabular, timeseries, text, and image data, yet no post-hoc method has been designed for classifiers that learn from multimodal inputs. Existing explainability methods for multimodal classifiers apply modality-specific attribution independently to each input, which fails to yield coherent explanations and does not transfer to arbitrary pre-trained multimodal architectures. To address these shortcomings, we present CRANE (Counterfactual Retrieval via Agnostic Nearest-neighbour Explanations), the first post-hoc, model-agnostic framework for nearest-neighbour multimodal counterfactual generation that retrieves real training examples from the target class as candidate explanations, ensuring plausibility by construction. Across four heterogeneous datasets and three fusion strategies, we show that Multimodal Consensus Retrieval (MC-R), which intersects per-modality neighbour sets to identify instances jointly close to the query in every modality, emerges as the strongest multimodal strategy, achieving the best or joint-best outcome on three of four datasets while grounding every explanation in the joint training distribution. Unimodal strategies remain competitive when a single modality dominates the decision boundary.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages + references, 3 figures, accepted to DSAA 2026</p></details> |
| **[MMVistaReason: Toward Open-Data and Post-Training Recipes for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01352v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.01352v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open multimodal reasoning models have benefited from large-scale reasoning supervision, yet reliable post-training remains challenging due to uneven data quality, inefficient supervision construction, imbalanced difficulty, and cross-domain interference. We introduce MMVistaReason (MVR), an open-data post-training recipe with three components: (1) broader capability coverage across complementary Analytical and Real-World reasoning groups, emphasizing structured reasoning versus visual perception and spatial grounding; (2) efficient SFT and RL data construction, standardizing heterogeneous open data through staged cleaning and annotation, combining difficulty-aware cascaded teacher distillation with answer-likelihood-based trajectory selection to construct MVR-SFT-528K, and applying scale-specific frontier filtering for MVR-RL-63K; and (3) specialize-then-integrate training, which trains complementary RL experts and consolidates their capabilities through multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). Our analyses reveal a capacity-dependent interaction between supervision difficulty, trajectory quality, and model capacity: smaller students benefit more from selected supervision, while larger students are robust to trajectory variation and mixed-domain interference. Mixed-domain RL introduces benchmark-level negative transfer, whereas MOPD provides consistent capability integration, with the preferred KL direction varying across model scales. Across 15 multimodal benchmarks, MVR-4B achieves an average score of 72.8, outperforming Qwen3.5-9B (Instruct) and MMFineReason-8B while using about 70% fewer samples than MMFineReason. Scaling to 9B improves the average to 74.4, surpassing Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (Instruct). | **[Drift Says When, Budget Says How Much: Rate-Controlled KV Re-selection for Long-Form Multimodal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31433v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31433v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>KV cache compression for multimodal LLMs usually selects the visual tokens to keep once, at prefill, and reuses that selection for the whole generation. | **[FiRe: Fine-grained Multimodal Reasoning for Enhanced Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13491v5)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.13491v5) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid progress of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), unified MLLMs that jointly perform image understanding and generation have advanced significantly. | **[Secure Agentic AI and Multimodal Intelligence in 6G Communication Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30436v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30436v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sixth-generation (6G) communication systems are expected to support immersive, intent-driven, and latency-critical services through tight integration of multimodal intelligence, edge computing, and autonomous network control. | **[Multimodal Embodied Agents: A Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30108v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30108v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. | **[Generalized CycleGRPO for Fine-Grained Multimodal Understanding and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29552v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29552v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained multimodal intelligence requires models to both understand a designated region and localize it from language. Graph-aligned retrieval combines comparable building scenarios with explicit engineering relationships, while compatibility, contradiction, evidence-precedence, and abstention mechanisms constrain inference under partial observability. Recovery actions are derived from supported damage-consequence relationships, and downtime uncertainty is propagated through an action-dependency network using Monte Carlo simulation. The framework is demonstrated for tornado-affected low-rise residential buildings using reconnaissance and image-based evidence. Comparisons with BaselineRAG and Structured Baseline RAG show improved agreement for key building-condition and recovery outcomes, together with traceable engineering reasoning and preserved evidence provenance. | **[Dynamic Cross-Modal Prompt Generation for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10765v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.10765v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, yet real-world deployment often requires continual capability expansion across sequential tasks. Instead of selecting prompts from a fixed pool, DRAPE derives prompt queries from the textual instruction and cross-attends to visual patch features, producing prompts conditioned on query-image pairs that are prepended to the frozen LLM. To mitigate forgetting during sequential updates, DRAPE applies null-space gradient projection to the shared projector and uses CLIP-based prototype routing for task-label-free generator selection at inference. Code is available at: https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/NeurIPS2026-Drape.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurlPS 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/NeurIPS2026-Drape</p></details> |
| **[GAMF-Net: Grouped Availability-Aware Multimodal Fusion for Smartphone Skin Lesion Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28129v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28129v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Smartphone clinical images provide a low-cost data source for computer-aided skin lesion classification in resource-constrained settings, but images alone cannot fully capture patients' medical histories and lesion symptoms. GAMF-Net achieves an Accuracy of $74.17\pm2.85\%$, a balanced accuracy (BACC) of $70.11\pm2.11\%$, a Macro-F1 of $68.85\pm0.85\%$, and a Macro-AUROC of $93.42\pm1.20\%$, outperforming all image-only and multimodal baselines reimplemented under a unified protocol. | **[Teaching the Way, Not the Answer: Privileged Tutoring Distillation for Multimodal Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07000v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.07000v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent post-training methods, particularly Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), have significantly enhanced the reasoning ability of Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). To address these limitations, we propose PTD-PO, a Privileged Tutoring Distillation Policy Optimization framework for RLVR that provides dense guidance without exposing the answer to the student policy. | **[Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21403v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21403v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. | **[Multimodal-Conditioned Latent Diffusion Models for Spatiotemporal Brain MRI Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25481v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25481v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatiotemporal forecasting of 3D anatomical changes is a critical generative vision challenge for modeling Alzheimer's Disease (AD) progression. | **[Media Intelligence Platform: Multimodal Detection of Synthetic and Manipulated Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27974v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27974v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid progress of generative artificial intelligence has led to a significant increase in manipulated multimedia content, including synthetic faces, forged videos, AI-generated images, fake audio, and locally edited images. | **[HypTI: Hyperbolic Geometry-Aware Multimodal Feature Fusion Framework for Dementia Detection Based on Tabular-Image Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27941v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27941v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-assisted dementia detection usually requires multimodal fusion of diverse data, such as clinical tabular assessments and neuroimaging data. | **[MMEnterprise-Bench: Evaluating Enterprise Agents over Evolving Multimodal Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27911v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27911v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise agents answer questions over workplace records that evolve through team collaboration. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce MMEnterprise-Bench, a benchmark comprising 1,613 questions grounded in evolving multimodal records from simulated collaboration projects, covering three categories: Evidence Reasoning, Conflict Resolution, and Unanswerability. We evaluate 10 proprietary and open-source multimodal LLMs and find that even the strongest models struggle when evidence contains cross-source or temporal conflicts or spans multiple images. These findings highlight the persistent challenges of reasoning over evolving workplace records and establish MMEnterprise-Bench as a testbed for developing more reliable enterprise agents. | **[Yuvion VL: A Multimodal Foundation Model for Adversarial Content and AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25034v3)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.25034v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose models often struggle to reliably identify and understand real-world multimodal risks, largely due to the inherent multimodal adversarial nature of content and AI safety. We further introduce Confuse-then-Contrast Fine-Tuning, a contrastive framework that mines model-specific confusions and constructs multi-image contrastive groups to enforce explicit discrimination of fine-grained visual-semantic elements, enabling the model to distinguish between visually similar cases with different safety implications in adversarial safety tasks. | **[Staged Continual Adaptation of Multimodal Foundation Models for Japanese Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27904v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27904v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Staged post-training of multimodal foundation models chains vision-language alignment, reasoning distillation, and domain tuning, yet how each phase trades capabilities against the others remains under-characterised. | **[A Survey of Generative Categories and Techniques in Multimodal Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10016v4)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2506.10016v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal generative models (MGMs) have rapidly expanded beyond text generation to encompass images, music, video, human motion, and 3D objects. Selected within-study comparisons reported in the reviewed literature support four findings: preference gains need not improve text relevance, and reward optimisation can reduce within-prompt diversity; intermediate plans require tests of their realisation in final outputs; expert granularity changes the mechanism and its storage, computation, and runtime costs; and gains against an optimisation score need confirmation outside that scoring path. | **[Reusing Multimodal Agents: Efficient Evaluation of Sound Designs in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27670v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27670v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper introduces a novel framework for efficiently evaluating in-game sound designs using multimodal learning and reinforcement learning. | **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27644v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. | **[TriGeoBench: A Multimodal Mathematical Geometry Benchmark with Figures and TikZ](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27626v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27626v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models show strong vision-language capabilities, yet their geometry-centric reasoning remains insufficiently understood. | **[BEACON: Bridging Multimodal Semantics and Asynchronous Propagation for Cloud System Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27542v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27542v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern large-scale cloud systems continuously generate massive and multimodal observability data, such as time-series metrics, log records, and system dependency graphs, which reflect the health state of the system. To ensure reliable cloud system operation, anomaly detection models identify abnormal states by analyzing these multimodal observations. However, existing approaches rely on simple fusion strategies and graph learning, ignoring the semantic gap among modalities and the asynchronous spatio-temporal dependencies across system instances over time. To this end, we propose BEACON, a framework to Bridge multimodal sEmantics and Asynchronous propagation for Cloud system anOmaly detectioN. Specifically, we introduce a Dynamic Interpretable Multimodal Aggregator (DIMA) that leverages an information-bottleneck mechanism to compress information from the semantics and temporal dynamics of metrics, and aligns them with logs in a shared prototype space. Furthermore, we propose an Asynchronous Spatio-Temporal Propagator (ASTP) that captures temporal dependencies with multi-scale temporal attention and models spatial relations with topology-aware aggregation. This enables asynchronous propagation of anomalies across system instances. Extensive experiments on three real-world cloud system datasets demonstrate that \textbf{BEACON} consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in anomaly detection.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDE 2027</p></details> |
| **[PartLLM: A Unified Multimodal Foundation for 3D Part Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25832v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.25832v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Part segmentation is a fundamental problem in computer graphics and 3D vision. Recent works have expanded 3D part segmentation beyond fixed taxonomies, but existing approaches typically only address a specific setting, such as text-guided part segmentation or point-based interaction. In this work, we argue that these settings can be unified as an intent-conditioned generative problem, where different prompts specify the desired part decomposition. To this end, we introduce PartLLM, a unified multimodal model that formulates 3D part segmentation as autoregressive semantic decomposition. Conditioned on an input shape and a user prompt, PartLLM autoregressively generates semantic part hypotheses as queries for mask prediction and feeds them to a decomposition-aware decoder that jointly predicts coherent part masks. This unified design supports text-guided part segmentation, interactive segmentation, and full-shape semantic decomposition with controllable granularity within a single model. Extensive experiments across these task settings show that PartLLM consistently outperforms task-specific baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of unifying 3D part segmentation under an intent-conditioned generative formulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to SIGGRAPH Asia 2026 (ACM Transactions on Graphics). Project Page: https://czvvd.github.io/PartLLMPage/</p></details> |
| **[Training-Free Continual Learning for Multimodal Forecasting via Prompt Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27468v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27468v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting a forecasting system to a changing world classically means retraining. We ask whether the task of continually learning regimes for time series forecasting can be represented by a piece of text: a guidance block, evolved by outcome-driven reflection and used in a frozen LLM forecaster's prompt. On a refreshed multimodal benchmark (weekly search indices, daily currencies, daily commodities, each window paired with web-retrieved news), we run GEPA-style prompt evolution under strict contamination control to prevent the data leakage common in timeseries benchmarks: the forecaster's knowledge cutoff predates every evaluated window. A 900-word evolved guidance carried by Llama-3.3-70B beats TimeSeries Foundation Models (TSFMs) on held-out data (GM MASE 0.776 vs. 0.898 for TabPFN-TS-3), transfers to more recent models without measurable loss, and costs dollars rather than GPU-days to update. We characterize the loop: out-of-loop gains concentrate in the first reflection, in-loop improvements overstate out-of-loop gains 2-3x unless an untouched monitor arbitrates, and error-only objectives silently suppress the text modality. We provide two extensions. First, compiling the guidance into executable code with a TSFM as a callable tool recovers most of the gain deterministically. Second, ablations show the advantage widens at short horizons and that removing the news text does not hurt the guided forecaster, consistent with the text's value being distilled into policy during learning. We argue that continual prompt evolution is a practical, training-free alternative to retraining for multimodal forecasting.</p></details> |  |
| **[Look Before You Reason: Implicit Visual Thinking for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27341v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27341v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have advanced rapidly in multimodal reasoning, yet they remain limited on tasks requiring fine-grained understanding of small or easily overlooked image regions. While effective, their multi-turn interactions with VLMs introduce redundant token computation and slow inference, and separate region inspection from the reasoning step that depends on it. To address these issues, we propose Look Before You Reason (LoRe), an implicit visual thinking framework for efficient visual reasoning within a single-turn VLM interaction. Given an image and a question, LoRe predicts the most relevant image region, aligns it with the corresponding image patch features, and strengthens the associated key-value (KV) cache entries, enabling subsequent decoding to directly exploit the selected visual cues without additional visual inputs or explicit tool calls. We further employ reinforcement learning to guide the VLM toward identifying informative image regions and leveraging them during reasoning. To support fine-grained evaluation of multimodal reasoning, we introduce COCO-ObjQA, a large-scale benchmark for object-centric multimodal reasoning. Experiments on three challenging multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that LoRe consistently improves VLM reasoning performance with low additional inference overhead.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Project Page: https://zerchen.github.io/projects/lore.html</p></details> |
| **[MuteBench: Modality Unavailability Tolerance Evaluation for Incomplete Multimodal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15235v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.15235v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal physiological data powers clinical AI systems from intensive care units to wearable devices, but sensors routinely fail in practice. Two failure modes are common: modality missing, where an entire channel is absent, and within-modality missing, where a contiguous time segment is lost. We present MuteBench, a benchmark covering 9 datasets from 7 clinical domains, 6 fusion architectures, and 2 missing-data modes over 125,000 samples. Through this benchmark, we find that architecture family is the strongest predictor of robustness, outweighing parameter count. Channel-independent models tolerate modality missing well but can be sensitive to within-modality missing, especially on short sequences. Curriculum modality dropout protects reliably only up to the maximum dropout rate used in training. We also find that channel count, sequence length, and modality alignment jointly determine which failure mode poses the greater threat. Finally, a PTB-XL case study suggests that diffusion-based imputation can improve downstream classification under within-modality missing, with the largest gains for models whose expert routing is most sensitive to corrupted inputs, though broader validation across datasets remains an open direction. MuteBench provides practitioners with concrete guidance for both selecting existing architectures and informing the design of future robust multimodal fusion methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[Design and Control of a Multimodal Legged-Aerial Robot Through Separate Control Contraction Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27315v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27315v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of dynamic legged locomotion and aerial mobility offers versatility that is deterred by challenges in payload distribution, actuation, and the contrasting requirements of both domains. We search for separate domain-specific contraction metrics for walking and flight independently offline and unify them using an online energy-based Control Lyapunov Function Quadratic Program (CLF QP). This enables optimal, cross-domain control allocation, which we show using simulations. | **[Generalizing Multimodal Foundational Models in Context-Driven Class Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27298v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27298v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) aims to learn new classes continuously without forgetting the previously learned ones. Specifically, we leverage their dense audio and visual representations and employ an attention-based contextual guidance module, in which the audio representations serve as task-stable contextual guidance for the visual representations, enabling effective class recognition in the incremental setting. To further mitigate catastrophic forgetting and preserve stability across tasks, we employ feature- and logit-level distillation, which are primarily responsible for the reduced forgetting. Extensive evaluations across various audio-visual CIL benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Code is available at https://github.com/avigupta2798/MFM-CIL.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work has been accepted in WACV 2027</p></details> |
| **[TokenSwap: Benchmarking and Reducing the Modality Gap in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28640v3)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.28640v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) should exhibit cross-modal consistency, generating consistent responses given semantically equivalent inputs across modalities. We introduce TokenSwap, a method that constructs such inputs by replacing textual concepts with semantically aligned images, resulting in sequences where visual tokens are interleaved with text tokens. Based on TokenSwap, we transform existing text-based benchmarks (e.g., MMLU) into image-interleaved counterparts, resulting in TokenSwap-Bench. Across 42 MLLMs, we observe a pervasive modality gap, with performance decreasing by 4.2% to 47.4% when moving from text-only to image-interleaved inputs, averaging 19.6% +/- 3.3% across models. Notably, reasoning models exhibit consistently smaller gaps, achieving an average gap of 10.1% compared to 25.5% for non-reasoning models, suggesting greater robustness in multimodal reasoning across equivalent textual and visual representations. In contrast, neither prompting strategies nor scaling training compute alone reliably reduces the modality gap. Finally, we demonstrate that incorporating TokenSwap during training effectively mitigates this gap, improving cross-modal alignment while preserving strong text-only and vision-language model performance. | **[MMedAgent-Plus: Scaling Multimodal Medical Agents across Modalities, Tasks and Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27233v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27233v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical workflows often require multiple interdependent analyses, but existing specialist and generalist AI models are not designed to coordinate them. | **[CARLAR Influence: A Structured Hallucination Risk in Multimodal Cheque Extraction and an Exploratory Compressed-Image Countermeasure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27192v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27192v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for structured extraction from financial documents, but their behavior under visual ambiguity is poorly understood. | **[HarnessIR: Harnessing Multimodal Foundation Models for Universal Real-World Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10133v3)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.10133v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world low-quality images suffer from complex mixed degradations, including but not limited to noise, blur, atmospheric effects, etc. Recent agentic methods usually model real-world image restoration (Real-IR) as a sequential tool calling problem over task-specific single-degradation restoration models. This paradigm, however, is fundamentally limited because complex real-world degradations cannot be cleanly undone degradation by degradation, and the tool used for task-specific models caps the capability of the agent system. In this work, we present HarnessIR, an agentic framework for Real-IR by harnessing a multimodal foundation model (MFM) as the executor. HarnessIR consists of five stages: perception and diagnosis, on-demand tool invocation, prompt composition, execution, and verification-driven refinement. Unlike prior agentic Real-IR methods that rely on tool chains assembled from task-specific models, HarnessIR feeds the restoration requirements, the perceptual diagnosis, and the evidence into an MFM that performs restoration in a single pass, followed by verification stages to determine whether the result warrants further processing. Under our harness, off-the-shelf MFMs handle restoration tasks remarkably well, achieving state-of-the-art results on the widely used MiO100 synthetic benchmark. More importantly, by exploiting the strong generalization ability of MFMs, HarnessIR delivers compelling restoration quality on challenging real-world scenes where previous agentic IR systems often struggle. Codes is available at https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/HarnessIR.</p></details> |  |
| **[EDMA: Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27120v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27120v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal question answering (MMQA) is based on integrating heterogeneous data sources, selectively leveraging relevant modalities, and ignoring distractors. Existing approaches based on Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) improve performance by splitting the task into intermediate steps, but do not quantify how each modality contributes to reaching the final answer. Thus, we propose Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering (EDMA), which uses logical entropy to formalise multimodal QA as an iterative process of entropy reduction and to quantify the contribution of each modality in distinguishing correct from incorrect candidate answers. This enables the construction of quantifiable answering trajectories, where each step is associated with a measurable entropy reduction. EDMA introduces (i) modality-conditioned partitioning to estimate unimodal entropy reduction, (ii) entropy-driven multimodal fusion to capture complementary information across modalities, and (iii) entropy-based selection of the answering trajectory that minimises the most logical entropy. Evaluated across standard MMQA benchmarks, EDMA outperforms competitive prompting baselines, with average gains of 12.8 F1 and 4.6 LLM-J points over Planning Prompting and 18.6 F1 and 8.3 LLM-J points over Chain-of-Thought. | **[OpenVLHarness: Real World Visual Agentic Tool-Use with Multimodal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26860v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26860v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress on visual reasoning, yet many tasks require evidence that is difficult to obtain through internal reasoning alone: our controlled study on 22 benchmarks shows that simply increasing the inference budget of the base model yields only modest gains. The capability layer combines task-level tool interfaces with evidence rendering, providing consistent access to heterogeneous backends and converting their outputs into model-readable observations. Persistent multimodal memory retains the corresponding images, structured data, and web references as addressable artifacts throughout each query. A coding interface executes generated programs over stored data, enabling computations that combine results from multiple tools. Together, these mechanisms support an iterative process of acquiring, inspecting, and composing multimodal evidence. Evaluated on 22 benchmarks with eight open and proprietary backbones, OpenVLHarness improves average scores by 9.7-12.6 points over matched no-tool baselines, including +11.2 with Qwen3-VL-8B, +9.7 with Kimi K3, and +9.9 with GPT-6 Astra. | **[Drive2Gauss: FeedForward 4D Gaussian Reconstruction from Multimodal Generative Driving Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26585v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26585v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating reusable driving environments requires both controllable future observations and an explicit representation that supports rendering from different viewpoints, yet existing methods still recover explicit scenes from generated RGB videos through optimization for each sequence. | **[From Generative to Discriminative: Lightweight Adaptation of SpeechLLMs for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20081v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.20081v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>SpeechLLMs can recognise emotion by generating a label token, but this approach relies on the LLM's existing output representations. | **[MOSS: Multimodal Origami Swamp Robot System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23813v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.23813v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic systems for environmental monitoring and ecological surveying in wetlands and swamps must operate across diverse terrestrial and aquatic conditions. D.2</p></details> |
