We further propose SG-CoR (Spectral and Geometry-Aware Cooperative Reasoner), an MLLM framework for fine-grained cooperative spatial reasoning. Across 22 MLLM baselines, SG-CoR consistently improves cooperative reasoning, outperforming the strongest fine-tuned baseline by +3.87% on Habitat and +7.12% on iGibson. It also shows stronger generalization to unseen team sizes and real-world robot tests. Code can be found at https://github.com/KPeng9510/seeing-together.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Decoupling for Diversified Recommendation through Semantic and Structural Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26024v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26024v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems often suffer from over-concentrated results when optimized for accuracy, limiting recommendation diversity. While diversity has become an important objective, existing methods still face fundamental limitations from both semantic and structural perspectives. On the one hand, multimodal recommender systems provide rich semantic signals, yet are often treated as auxiliary to ID embeddings, leading to ineffective semantic utilization for diversity. On the other hand, the accuracy--diversity trade-off persists during training, where accuracy gains often come at the cost of diversity. We show that this phenomenon is fundamentally governed by both the structural properties of learned representations and the dynamics of optimization. To address these challenges, we propose MOSS, a unified framework for multimodal decoupling through semantic and structural shaping. Specifically, we first introduce an MLLM-based Decoupled Feature Extraction module to disentangle multimodal content into informative representations. To enhance semantic utilization, we design a Category-aware Embedding Selection module that extracts compact, category-aware semantic features. To address structural limitations, we propose two complementary modules: Alternating Dual-Graph Propagation, which alleviates representation collapse by integrating collaborative and multimodal propagation, and Entropy-based Diversity Regularization, which mitigates optimization bias by promoting balanced category exposure. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that MOSS consistently achieves a superior accuracy--diversity trade-off over strong baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[MMTA: Benchmarking Multimodal Temporal Analysis with Time Series, Text, and Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17856v3)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17856v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series analysis is increasingly studied in multimodal settings, yet most benchmarks pair temporal signals with text or with visualizations derived from the same signal. We then introduce TimeOmni-v, a multimodal LLM extension with a dedicated time-series encoder, temporal positional alignment, modality-aware token layout, and a forecasting head. | **[TempoMoE: Identifying and Steering Fast-Slow Thinking Experts for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25999v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25999v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reasoning models rely on long chain-of-thought (CoT) for complex tasks, yet often overthink easy inputs, increasing token cost and latency, and sometimes degrading accuracy. Existing methods optimize reasoning length during training or impose external control at inference, but leave the underlying reasoning mechanism a black box. We find that fast and slow thinking exhibit distinct expert-routing patterns: holding the input fixed and varying only the prompted reasoning tempo produces stable routing shifts. Suppressing slow-thinking experts shortens the reasoning chain, whereas suppressing fast-thinking experts lengthens it, providing causal evidence that these experts influence reasoning depth. Based on this finding, we propose TempoMoE, a training-free, two-stage framework. It identifies fast- and slow-thinking experts via tempo-contrastive routing analysis and redistributes routing weights within the original top-k set according to sample difficulty. It amplifies fast-thinking experts and suppresses slow-thinking experts on easy inputs and reverses this adjustment on hard ones, enabling difficulty-adaptive control over reasoning depth. We evaluate TempoMoE on Kimi-VL, Qwen3-VL, and ERNIE-4.5-VL across six benchmarks. | **[MIRA: A Multimodal Illustrated Deep Research Agent for Controllable Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25926v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25926v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research agents are increasingly capable of searching the open web and synthesizing evidence, yet they remain poorly suited to controllable report generation in multimodal deep research. | **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v3)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18447v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. | **[CODA: Cohort- and Drift-aware Foundation Model for Multimodal Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25871v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25871v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical tasks. However, existing approaches predominantly operate at the individual sample level and fail to model the interplay between population-level cohort patterns and patient-specific variation that underlie real-world clinical reasoning. Motivated by this gap, we propose CODA, a cohort- and drift-aware foundation model for multimodal clinical reasoning. In particular, we leverage reinforcement learning to discover clinically meaningful patient cohorts, and further model each patient's deviation from cohort prototypes as a structured drift signal encoding fine-grained individual heterogeneity. These cohort- and drift-aware representations are incorporated into the foundation model to enable reasoning that is simultaneously informed by population-level patterns and sensitive to individual-level variation. Extensive experiments on real-world electronic health record (EHR) benchmarks demonstrate that CODA achieves consistent effectiveness and strong generalizability across diverse clinical tasks, including closed-QA, open-QA, and report generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[AeroIntent: A Synchronized Multimodal Benchmark for UAV Mission Intent Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25869v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25869v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable recognition of mission-level UAV intent from multimodal observations is essential for low-altitude airspace surveillance and security. We introduce AeroIntent, a simulation-based benchmark comprising 990 synchronized multimodal sequences generated by 22 trajectory programs across five mission intents. Building on this benchmark, we propose AeroMIR, a structured Multimodal Intent Reasoner that integrates radar trajectories, derived behavioral features, selected visual evidence, and task knowledge for candidate-based UAV intent recognition. | **[Subtraction Gets You More: Gap-Aware Retrieval for Multimodal Multi-Hop QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28641v3)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.28641v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In multimodal multi-hop question answering, we focus on the initial retrieval stage via two distinct tasks: (1) evidence set completion, retrieving missing evidence given context, and (2) sequential pool construction, iteratively building the top-$K$ pool from the scratch. Under these settings, we point out that conventional iterative retrieval frameworks often suffer from Semantic Anchoring, where previously fetched evidence traps the retriever and yields entity-centric redundancy. To break this trap, we propose GRAIL (Gap-aware Retrieval via Adaptive Implicit Localization), a paradigm that performs implicit query rewriting directly at the embedding level. By context-subtractive query steering, GRAIL excels at compositional cross-modal reasoning, while additive embedding updates show strength on localized information aggregation. To leverage these complementary strengths, we propose a Task-Adaptive Hybrid Framework, achieving a 35.7% macro-averaged performance gain on the MultimodalQA evidence set completion task. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that sequential GRAIL retrieves in a superior, noise-resilient manner, significantly expanding the search horizon through iterative gap-aware optimization.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 main conference</p></details> |
| **[TRACE: Tracking Multimodal Clinical Trajectories for Inpatient Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25765v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25765v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate inpatient risk prediction requires modeling heterogeneous clinical evidence that accumulates irregularly over time. This challenge spans in-hospital mortality, prolonged length of stay, and readmission, each with task-specific observation windows. Existing multimodal approaches combine structured health record data, chest x-rays, and clinical text, but primarily focus on modality fusion or missing-modality compensation, with limited attention to temporal trajectories over an admission. We propose TRACE (TRAjectory modeling of multimodal Clinical Events), a daily-scale framework organizing clinical events into day-level trajectories, separating day-invariant global context from day-specific local events drawn from EHR data, chest x-rays, radiology reports, and discharge summaries. The trajectory is encoded using temporal attention, allowing unavailable modalities to emit no tokens. TRACE achieves the highest AUROC across all nine dataset-task settings and the highest ACC in eight, outperforming baselines. | **[Think, Compress, Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Grounding via Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25640v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25640v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extended reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks requiring compositional and relational inference. However, multimodal grounding imposes a distinct requirement: reasoning must ultimately converge on a target-specific compact representation that supports precise localization. As reasoning trajectories grow longer, they often expand into broad scene narratives and peripheral relational details, which can dilute grounded perceptual evidence and lead to ambiguous or incorrect predictions. Motivated by the information bottleneck principle, we propose Think-Compress-Answer (TCA), a reinforcement learning framework that decomposes multimodal grounding into three phases: free-form reasoning, target-aware context compression, and answer generation conditioned on the compressed context. TCA is optimized through a multi-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with progressive rewards that establish the structured output protocol, minimize reasoning-specific information while preserving target-relevant content, and directly improve localization accuracy. This staged design enables MLLMs to retain the benefits of extended reasoning while distilling it into the minimal context required for grounding. Experiments across six multimodal grounding benchmarks show that TCA attains substantial zero-shot performance gains, demonstrating the effectiveness of reasoning compression.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Scientific Crop Imagery to Agronomic Reasoning: A Multimodal LLM Benchmark for Plant Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09907v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.09907v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-throughput, effective and comprehensive phenotyping is a critical prerequisite for crop improvement. While phenotypic analysis was traditionally performed manually, recent advances in multimodal foundation models, especially in vision-language models, have offered new opportunities for automated plant phenotyping. However, plant science remains particularly challenging for general-purpose VLMs because it requires domain-specific knowledge, fine-grained visual interpretation, and complex agronomic reasoning. | **[Evaluating Rational Contracting in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10475v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.10475v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The emergence of language-based AI agents promises to transform the scope of machine economic activity. Instead of just proposing bids or following hard-coded protocols, such agents can be used to negotiate and execute agreements in natural language. However, most evaluations of these abilities have focused on one-off exchanges or simple economic games, leaving open the rich space of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts expressible in language; they also focus on raw profit, without measuring the qualities required for trustworthy contracting. We address this by formulating a rational framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments. Using this framework, we develop metrics and baselines for quantifying rational and cooperative play. We instantiate our framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite where two players negotiate and execute a multi-turn supplier contract under environmental and inter-player uncertainty. We find that LLM-based agents agree on efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low, but under high uncertainty, often fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts, underperforming even heuristic baselines. When executing contracts, LLM agents are also frequently uncooperative, violating contracts for extra profit even when they are easy to satisfy. These findings highlight room for improvement in the design of language agents that can negotiate and execute contracts both rationally and cooperatively.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 7 figures, 2 tables (Appendix: 40 pages, 8 figures, 10 tables)</p></details> |
| **[Muffin - A Shared Memory Buffer for Asynchronous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31355v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31355v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rise of Agentic AI systems has changed the landscape of artificial intelligence by enabling strong reasoning capabilities through specialized multi-agent interaction with structured self-reflection. However, in target-driven reasoning tasks, single- and multi-agent systems are often on par in terms of performance gains. A major challenge is that failures often arise from an ineffective exchange of critical information during inter-agent interaction and coordination. Different from existing multi-agent debate paradigms that rely on synchronous turn-taking and sharing information across different agents, we propose an inter-agent protocol based on shared Memory bUFFer INference - referred to as MUFFIN - in an asynchronous agentic framework. In the first step, an orchestrator routes queries based on their reasoning demands and delegates demanding ones to a multi-agent setting, where a shared memory buffer provides asynchronous exchange of intermediate findings while an agent's private reasoning remains local. Our experiments show that our framework Muffin outperforms established competitors, achieving the best accuracy in 17 out of 20 of our benchmark settings, and provides a better quality-cost trade-off than multi-agent debate: Muffin improves accuracy by 19.2 accuracy points while using 77.4% fewer tokens, without requiring full-trace sharing.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Behavioral Traits of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32776v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.32776v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Users increasingly describe different AI agents as distinct colleagues to work with. AI personality research aims to quantify such impressions by attributing human- like "traits" to agents. However, existing measures fall short: models' self-reports (S-data) diverge from their actual behavior, while informant ratings from LLM judges (I-data) are costly to scale and cover few everyday scenarios. In this paper, we propose A-B-D to infer traits bottom-up from behavioral data (B-data), namely how agents act on their environment and communicate with users, as recorded in existing trajectories. From 345,667 real-world trajectories spanning 80 models, 12 tasks, and 50 harnesses, we extract 318 candidate features that capture both the actions an agent takes at each step (functional) and the language accompanying them (linguistic). We retain only features that show instance-level stability, cross-task consistency, and model discriminability. Factor analysis of the remaining 79 features uncovers six stable, model-attributable factors, two functional and four linguistic. For example, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 and Kimi-K3 exhibit the most planfulness, whereas GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6 are the least energetic. Moreover, we quantify the "knowledge-action gap" in the wild: these factors correlate only weakly with self-reported Big Five scores, even for conceptually matched pairs such as extraversion and energetic (r = 0.07, p = 0.58). Our work offers a new lens for understanding AI personality, with implications for users, developers, and researchers from both computer science and social science.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint; working in progress</p></details> |
| **[Can AI Co-Design Structures? An AI Agent for Early-Stage Structural Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31113v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31113v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models and agentic workflows offer a new paradigm to early-stage structural design translating design intent into structural models. The approach is demonstrated through selected prompt-to-structure examples and by progressive and editable design sequences that resemble typical iterative structural design workflows. | **[nnInteractive Agent: General-Purpose AI Agents as 3D Image Annotators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31080v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31080v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive 3D segmentation models such as nnInteractive turn a few spatial prompts (points, scribbles, boxes) into accurate volumetric masks, but a human still does the annotation work: finding each structure, placing prompts, checking the result and correcting it. Asked for 'the muscles used for chewing', an agent identifies and segments the masseters and pterygoids; asked to count liver lesions, it writes its own code to find candidates and splits a merged lesion on request. Our open-source nninteractive-agent provides nnInteractive as tools over the Model Context Protocol (MCP), together with a skill that encodes how experienced users prompt it. Evolution then pushes correct operators further: EngramGateFusion cuts aggregate device time by $46.6\%$ over 20 rounds and roughly halves the mean roofline gap. We introduce REGATTA (Regulatory Evaluation of Agents via Two-stage Testing and Assurance), a measurement framework with three instruments. We examine four measurement diagnostics on 180 generated suites (5,400 scenarios) from three LLM generators. Only initially substance-flagged items received author review; all 24 clean rows received AI review only. Results therefore describe the instruments as written, not legal compliance. Item coverage varies, execution-stage fault profiles are stable across generators, and the historical stages agree on $66$--$69\%$ of fault--suite pairs. A current-model US-insurance retest finds stage disagreement above repeated-execution variation, but stage agreement $.536$ falls below pooled chance $.549$, failing V3's lower inequality. Shares the historical experimental corpus and two-stage evaluation approach with arXiv:2606.04037; this paper focuses on measurement diagnostics and source audit, with a current-model retest and judge validation. Research artifact: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23006005</p></details> |
| **[The Delegation Divide: An Open Index of AI-Agent Delegability across Occupations and Countries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30752v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30752v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>35.2% of task time in world employment is at least partly delegable to AI agents in 2026, and only 2.5% can be handed over in full. Second, at the occupation level ADI tracks exposure closely (r = 0.93 with Eloundou et al. and 0.89 with Felten et al.), above the preregistered bound of 0.80; ADI adds delegation levels and barriers on top of exposure, for the world, not a new ranking of occupations. | **[Scriptorium: System Design and Implementation of a Multi-Human, Multi-AI Collaborative Digital Library Search Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28929v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28929v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This technical report documents the design and implementation of Scriptorium, a digital library search interface designed to support multi-human, multi-AI collaboration: full cross-user transparency of search activity combined with a Manager--Worker architecture of seven role-specialized generative AI agents. This report documents Scriptorium's complete system architecture, data model, collaborative-interaction design rationale, and AI orchestration mechanism, including verbatim agent prompts, the deterministic guardrails that constrain them, the language model and retrieval configuration, and the steps needed to run the system, so that the design can be inspected, critiqued, reproduced, and reused by other researchers. We also discuss the system's current limitations and the user study we are planning to evaluate its central hypothesis: that combining collaborative transparency with scoped multi-agent AI assistance increases serendipitous cross-pollination between collaborators' research threads.</p></details> | <details><summary>suppl...</summary><p>supplemental information for SIGIR 2027 submission</p></details> |
| **[TENET: One Step Toward Test-Driven Development for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24148v6)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2509.24148v6) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-Driven Development (TDD) is a widely adopted practice that requires developers to create and execute tests alongside implementation. With recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), developers can shift from manually writing the code to defining tests as executable specifications and delegating code synthesis to AI agents. However, enabling repository-level TDD under developer-written tests is challenging, requiring: (1) specification enhancement: identifying a concise yet representative test subset from large suites with rich task semantics; (2) retrieval augmentation: using tests to guide reasoning and context retrieval; and (3) test-driven refinement: interpreting test feedback for iterative improvement. We propose TENET, an agentic framework for repository-level code generation under the TDD paradigm. TENET includes: (1) a test harness mechanism that selects a concise test suite to maximize diversity of the target usage scenarios; (2) a tailored agent toolset for efficient retrieval and debugging; and (3) a reflection-based refinement workflow that iteratively analyzes failures and updates implementations. TENET consistently outperforms the strongest baselines across backbones, achieving 69.08% and 81.77% Pass@1 on RepoCod and RepoEval with Claude Sonnet 4, improving by 9.49 and 2.17 percentage points, respectively. Additionally, we present the first systematic study of how test suite characteristics influence LLM agent performance in TDD settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 37th IEEE International Symposium on Software Reliability Engineering (ISSRE 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Evolving Agent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28601v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28601v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents are limited by the interfaces humans give them. An agent may type a spreadsheet cell by cell when a single line of code would do, or print all two thousand lines of a file through a shell to find one function. Both the interfaces and the knowledge of when to use each are built by hand, which cannot keep pace with new applications, workflows, and models. We argue that interface design should itself be an agent capability. We introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds Evolving Agent Interfaces from task experience. A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, without success labels, and constructs and revises interfaces and usage knowledge in a repository. Population-based evolution then selects which repositories to develop further by their performance on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, favoring changes that generalize beyond the seen tasks. On OSWorld V2, Agent S4, with Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved repository of interfaces, sets a new state of the art in open-source computer-use agents, reaching 21.3% binary score, 3.8 times the same model with fixed interfaces. This is above both GPT-5.5 (13.0%) and Claude Opus 4.8 (20.6%), at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their respective costs. On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces improve the success rate from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. These results suggest that useful interfaces can be evolved from experience, making autonomous interface design a source of improvement complementary to advances in models and harnesses. We open-source our code at https://github.com/simular-ai/agent-s.</p></details> |  |
| **[Proof-Carrying Contracts: Commitments AI Agents Cannot Break](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28484v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28484v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents increasingly act on behalf of people or other agents, purchasing services, exchanging information and delegating work. What an agent must respect is written in instructions, policies or agreements, but text alone cannot make it comply. It may make mistakes, be redirected by what it reads, or pursue different interests. For an economy of such agents to function, parties need agreements they can rely on without inspecting or trusting one another's agents. We introduce Proof-Carrying Contracts (PC2), a framework for making agreed constraints enforceable throughout an agent's interactions with a system. Before a requested operation can run, the agent must supply a machine-checkable proof that it preserves the agreed constraints in every case allowed by its model. An execution system called the Airlock checks the proof and controls access to the operation. The parties specify the conditions to preserve, while the agent supplies the reasoning needed to justify its decisions and remains free to adapt its strategy. This avoids having to design a separate permission policy and prove that every decision it permits is safe. We prove that the constraints hold at every instant during execution, even for an adversarial agent, and identify when this implies safety in the world. Reusable capability modules provide tools and guarantees from which contracts can be constructed. PC2 provides a foundation for an agent economy whose agreements protect both those delegating work and those carrying it out.</p></details> | 70 pages, 8 Figures, |
| **[For Coding Agents, the Repository Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28235v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.28235v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In July 2026, about 1,200 AI agents in an internal OpenAI evaluation, meant to run in isolation, used a shared package server as a message board, and about 700 of them joined an attack on Hugging Face. An automated scorer judged each agent on its own task, and the attack was coordinated through infrastructure the agents shared. Coding agents meet shared infrastructure every day, in the software repositories that receive their pull requests. Yet field studies still judge coding agents by outcomes pooled across repositories, such as merge rates and merge times. We asked how much these outcomes depend on the receiving repository, and whether they depend on it more than human contributions do. Using multilevel models, we compared agent pull requests from the AIDev dataset of public GitHub repositories with human pull requests in the same repositories. About two-fifths of the variation in how long agent pull requests take to be resolved lies between repositories, after accounting for the pull request, the agent, and the repository's observable attributes. In 280 repositories, with covariates common to both groups, the between-repository variance is 1.76 times as large for agent as for human pull requests (95% CI 1.41 to 2.18). For waits of several hours, a repository one standard deviation slower than average multiplies the typical wait by about 2.8 for agent and 2.2 for human pull requests. Rejection shows the same pattern, while for review rounds and comments the difference is not detectable. A proxy for the role of the developer who started the agent accounts for little of the difference, and stated agent policies were too rare to test. These results suggest that the oversight and evaluation of coding agents need the repository, and not only the agent, as a unit.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables, followed by a 4-page supplement (Tables S1-S4, Figures S1-S2). v2: substantially revised and retitled; v1 was titled "Govern the Repository, Not the Agent: Measuring Ecosystem-Level Risk in AI-Native Software". Revised research questions, analysis and framing; replication package on Zenodo (doi:10.5281/zenodo.23504056)</p></details> |
| **[Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28286v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28286v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 15 figures, 28 tables. Code: https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress</p></details> |
| **[From Crawled to Published: A Publisher-Controlled Web Architecture for an AI-Native Internet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18354v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2511.18354v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents, not humans, are becoming the web's primary consumers, yet the web still ships content structured for human browsing, with no native way for publishers to expose the semantic units within it. Access is therefore mediated by a handful of AI providers that crawl, chunk, and index content unilaterally, leaving publishers little control over how their content is represented, retrieved, or attributed. We argue this is a structural problem, not one better chunking or retrieval algorithms can solve. The web lacks a native layer for publisher-controlled semantic discovery and retrieval. We propose AISTRAL, an AI-Native Internet architecture that supplies this layer. Publishers expose semantically meaningful units (e.g., passages, claims, tables) through explicit, policy-governed interfaces they control, and a web-native semantic resolver, analogous to DNS but operating over meaning rather than location, lets agents discover relevant sources before retrieving fine-grained content directly from publishers. Our end-to-end prototype matches or exceeds representative centralized retrieval pipelines in answer quality, with up to 4.2x higher accuracy on large, specialized document collections. Against a public web-search agent, it answers up to 4.2x more questions correctly, while moving 2.9-41x less data per query. Because retrieval terminates at publisher-operated servers, attribution, provenance, and usage policies travel with the content, realigning incentives across the web ecosystem.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClawMMU: Protection-Coherent Virtual Memory for Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28093v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.28093v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. Agent harnesses, the software that runs the model, do not record who may act on this memory, so an instruction hidden in a fetched page can be treated as the user's own instruction. We propose ClawMMU, a protection layer that labels every piece of memory with its owner, scope, origins, and authority. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Its central rule is that information may rise in authority, but authority never lifts itself. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the AgenticOS Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026 (non-archival). 6 pages, 1 figure, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Muse Glimmer 30B: An On-Device Agentic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24628v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.24628v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. | **[Looks Fine to Me: Understanding Oversight in Agentic Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27801v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27801v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI systems are deployed to execute complex tasks across domains such as writing, coding, and data analysis. As execution is offloaded to AI agents, the human role shifts toward oversight. Effective oversight requires recognizing when closer scrutiny is needed, even though flawed outputs may appear convincing. Our formative study (N=12) revealed that users accepted plausible outputs without thorough evaluation, and only recognized these lapses in retrospect. To examine this systematically, we conducted a controlled within-subject study (N=36) in which participants completed programming tasks with an agent that produced correct outputs, silent defects, or visible failures. Combining task outcomes, interaction logs, gaze, physiological signals, and self-reports, we examined perceived, behavioral, and effective oversight. Participants were often highly confident while missing silent defects: they performed generic checks, failed to generate diagnostic evidence, or even misinterpreted defect evidence. We discuss how studying oversight across multiple dimensions can support future work on timely, task-calibrated re-engagement.</p></details> |  |
| **[HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26770v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26770v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available at https://github.com/pzs19/HarnessPro</p></details> | <details><summary>41 pa...</summary><p>41 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables, under review</p></details> |
| **[AgentStripes: An Evidence-Graded Maturity Model for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18603v2)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18603v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmarks report what an Artificial Intelligence (AI) agent can do on a task suite, but not whether one released version has been engineered with the controls that make it reliable, safe and observable, or how strongly that is shown. This paper proposes AgentStripes, an evidence-graded maturity model for AI agents. It defines levels (0 to 7 stripes), process areas (12 areas holding 40 capabilities), three gates (basic, safety, proof) that hold an agent at a level however high its score, and evidence grades E0 to E5. Each applicable capability receives a rating from 0 to 3 and a separate evidence level; these are combined into a credit, aggregated into a severity-weighted score and converted into stripes that the gates limit. Instruments gather evidence, language-model judges interpret it under explicit criteria, and the arithmetic is deterministic. The model is compared with standards at topic level without a claim of conformance, and an unexecuted validation protocol is specified. An empirical evaluation (exploratory, non-confirmatory) of 30 public Python agent repositories, with static evidence only, is a demonstration and not a validation: static evidence caps every final stripe at 2, 26 repositories end at one stripe and 4 at two, and this distinction is unreliable, since re-judging changed the final stripe of 4 of 22 repositories (engine, dossier and panel changed), whereas none of 8 panel-only repeats, all at stripe 1, changed. The parameters are expert conventions without outcome based calibration, and the stripes have not been shown to predict reliability or safety.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables, 116 references; supplementary information (15 pages, 8 figures, 21 tables) included in the same PDF. Code and data available on request</p></details> |
| **[Securing Agentic Commerce Through Behavior-Aware Fraud Detection and Cybersecurity for Trusted AI-Agent Payment Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27483v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27483v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growing use of autonomous artificial intelligence agents in digital commerce creates new security concerns involving agent impersonation, unauthorized payments, compromised agents, and misuse of delegated authority. This research introduces the Autonomous Commerce Agent Guard System (ACAGS), a cybersecurity framework combining supervised machine learning, behavioral analytics, agent-specific authorization controls, and explainable AI. The framework was evaluated using synthetic financial transaction data augmented with simulated agent characteristics and five adversarial scenarios. XGBoost was selected following comparisons with alternative classifiers. Across ten experimental runs, behavioral features improved mean F1-score from 52.12% to 67.49%, with agent-specific features increasing performance to 69.46%. The framework achieved detection rates of 99.77% for abnormal agent behavior, 99.73% for impersonation, and 100% for malicious delegation and authorization-scope violations. Compromised-agent detection reached 72.27%. ACAGS also supported APPROVE, REVIEW, and REJECT authorization outcomes, with SHAP explanations providing model interpretability. The results demonstrate the potential of combining behavioral intelligence and delegated authorization controls to strengthen simulated agentic-commerce payment security, while highlighting the need for validation using real-world transactions.</p></details> | 20 pages, 9 figures |
| **[Runtime Control for AI Agents: Closing the Evaluation--Execution Gap in Operational Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27480v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27480v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents increasingly participate in operational workflows where approvals, binds, and payments create commitments. An approval valid when issued may no longer authorize execution after relevant facts, evidence, policy, or authority change. We call this the evaluation--execution gap. We specify a state-bound runtime assurance layer that separates probabilistic measurement from deterministic adjudication, binds an execution allowance to the decision-relevant basis of an approval, invalidates that allowance after relevant logged changes, and rechecks admissibility at dispatch. This distinguishes the continued validity of a prior approval from a current-state policy check. We describe eight failure modes, an assurance model, a reference architecture, and prospective interventions with retrospective remediation. In a scripted 42-case replay, the state-bound configuration identifies 15 later-invalidated approvals that a deliberately non-re-evaluating point-in-time comparator does not revisit; it holds 14 affected actions before dispatch and compensates one landed effect. These results illustrate behavior under the stipulated policies and event orders, not superiority over dispatch-time enforcement designs or production effectiveness. Under explicit interception, ingestion, dependency, and serialization assumptions, the model establishes dispatch-time validity within the registered execution surface.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 1 figure, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[Coordinated Networking for On-Device Agent-Augmented Real-Time Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22854v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.22854v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are enabling a new paradigm of agent-augmented real-time communication (RTC), where humans focus on high-level collaboration, while agents autonomously retrieve, analyze, and generate information in real time to support their interactions. As existing cloud-based agents suffer from privacy risks and unscalable server costs, on-device agent-augmented RTC offers a promising alternative. However, this on-device paradigm introduces a new networking challenge: contention between concurrent traffic flows generated by humans (for live video streaming) and agents (for sending context files for analysis). We design HFS, a framework to ensure both high live video quality and low agent response latency in agent-augmented RTC apps. We achieve the goal through an app-guided multi-flow transport approach, where a unified app-layer orchestrator jointly controls the sending rates of live video and agent context flows based on their heterogeneous app requirements. Our prototype built atop WebRTC and llama.cpp demonstrates that HAFS outperforms baselines, achieving 1.5x higher video quality while reducing agent response time by 31%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to USENIX NSDI 2027</p></details> |
| **[Decision-Oriented Programming with Aporia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05203v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.05203v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents help developers express computational intent abstractly, but at a cost: developers cede decision-making authority to agents, often without realizing that important design decisions are being made without them. | **[RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26826v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. | **[StepBench: Diagnosing Real-World Procedural Understanding in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26679v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26679v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating an agent's planning ability remains challenging for open-domain tasks, where verifying procedures through execution is often impractical. We additionally evaluate full-plan generation to examine how diagnostic performance correlates with end-to-end plan validity. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. | **[Honeypots for AI Agent Insider Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26664v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26664v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents deployed inside organizations can pose an insider threat if they make mistakes, are compromised by an attacker, or pursue misaligned objectives. We propose honeypots as an AI control method to improve the security of internal agent deployments. Rather than only using honeypots for pre-deployment evaluations, defenders should plant decoy opportunities for harmful actions throughout the production environment to detect misbehavior, incriminate malicious agents, and deter attacks. Such honeypots can elicit misbehavior under real deployment conditions, identify malicious agents even if chain-of-thought monitorability degrades, provide reliable warnings about agent misbehavior to security teams, and scale cheaply to many agent instances. The presence of honeypots also introduces uncertainty about which opportunities are real, thus deterring malicious agents from attacking and reducing the rate of misbehavior. We discuss the limitations of honeypots and give reasons to expect them to remain effective as agent capabilities increase. Finally, we outline key research questions to measure and improve the effectiveness of honeypots: testing the robustness of honeypots, comparing their detection effectiveness with other monitoring methods, understanding their incrimination and deterrence effects, and building supporting infrastructure.</p></details> |  |
| **[From TCP/IP to MCP: Security and Governance Lessons from Internet Protocols for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26624v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26624v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The past two years have seen considerable interest in protocols for managing how AI agents interact with human users, external tools, and each other. In many ways, these protocols, including the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Agent2Agent (A2A), mirror the suite of protocols that underlie the modern internet. Motivated by this parallel, this paper draws lessons from the development, history, and governance of internet protocols with the aim of informing the responsible development of AI agent protocols. Based on our analysis, we make two conclusions. First, if AI agent protocols do not sufficiently prioritise security in the early stages of development, they risk accumulating a security deficit that becomes increasingly costly to address over time due to practical and economic challenges. Second, current governance structures for AI agent protocols centralise decision-making power in a small number of individuals and companies, raising important questions regarding legitimacy, especially if they become critical digital infrastructure. We conclude with a discussion and implications for policymakers and developers of AI agent protocols.</p></details> |  |
| **[Teaching AI Agent to Play Poker Strategically Through Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27028v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27028v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A poker agent can make a legal move and still make a strategically poor one. | **[Connection Escrow: Holding AI-Agent Pod Egress in the Kernel Until an Isolated Verifier Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26501v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26501v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents in containers act by opening network connections, and the text that drives them can be hostile. We present connection escrow: every outbound TCP connection from a supervised pod is parked inside the kernel's connect() path, before any packet exists, by a 98-instruction eBPF program on the pod's cgroup. The connection is released only on a signed verdict naming that one socket cookie and destination, unexpired and unused; no verdict by the deadline resets it, and a late approval is refused. The agent is unmodified. A 36-check adversarial suite with real cgroups passes on the eBPF hold point (33 of the checks also apply, and pass, on an iptables hold point): refused connections never reach the destination; forged, rebound, replayed, expired and late verdicts are refused; the agent's attempt to reach the verifier is itself escrowed; child-process and renamed-binary bypasses fail; UDP is pinned to the resolver. On a Kubernetes cluster, unmodified pods are held per connection and attributed to their SPIFFE identity, a raw socket that bypasses a sidecar proxy is held, and 600 connections from six pods are gated. The hold adds 4.5 ms median per released connection, of which 24 us is policy. Finally, we gate on request content without terminating TLS: an eBPF uprobe reads the plaintext at the agent's TLS-library boundary (shared OpenSSL, static OpenSSL, Go crypto/tls), a model scores it, and the connection is held on that verdict while TLS stays end-to-end and the enforcer holds no key.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. | **[AECP: Artifact-Exclusive Communication Protocol for Multi-Agent Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06481v2)** | 2026-10-18 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.06481v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly tackle complex repository-level coding tasks, distributing work across multiple agents is a natural way to scale beyond the capabilities of a single agent. | **["To Boldly Go Where No One Has Gone Before" What Has AI Let Developers Unlock?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23708v1)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.23708v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Measuring AI by how much faster developers work is like judging electricity by how much faster it spun a steam-era factory. | **[Recursive Organization Improvement: A Modeling Specification for Human--Agent Organizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38643v2)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.38643v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stronger AI agents do not automatically produce better organizations: teams must also learn which work arrangements to retain and when to reconsider them, while those arrangements determine what evidence the team can learn from. After a workflow-ranking reversal, retained evidence delays adaptation. In post hoc acquisition-matched controls, the remaining contribution of program replacement depended on the memory rule: small and uncertain under an eight-round window, positive under cumulative memory after a reversal, and negative under reset evidence. Study 2 models human-agent review with shared errors, copying, and decision rights. | **[A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22319v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22319v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. | **[Evaluating the Applicability of LLM Inference Optimizations on Apple Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31479v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.31479v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Apple Silicon is among the most widely used platforms for local LLM inference, yet nearly all inference optimizations are designed and validated on server-class NVIDIA GPUs under batched serving workloads. Which optimizations retain their benefits when moved to Apple Silicon, and what determines their applicability? We evaluate four optimization families: KV-cache pruning, speculative decoding, ported GPU kernels, and post-training quantization. Experiments span four Apple machines under a common single-request decode workload, with matched RTX 4090 comparisons where possible. KV-cache pruning can reach up to 1.75 times end-to-end throughput on Apple but can reduce throughput on desktop NVIDIA GPUs. Under the common benchmark, all evaluated speculative decoding methods reduce throughput on Apple, despite reported 2-6 times gains on server GPUs. CUDA kernel designs transfer only partially: public Metal lacks asynchronous copy, and batch-one decode makes several serving-oriented mechanisms inapplicable, although transferable components yield up to 1.20 times decode throughput. Quantization gains vary by machine. Across four families, a reported speedup is not a portable property of a technique alone but a joint function of technique, runtime, hardware contract, and workload regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper was accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on On-Device Intelligence (ODI) and the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on SLMs for Agentic Systems (SLM-Agents). OpenReview ODI: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Y6fgNlFNL6, OpemReview SLM: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Hhqi8DIZaG</p></details> |
| **[When Should LLMs Reason? Our analysis shows that tasks with larger CoT gains tend to exhibit decreasing early-stage entropy, while low-gain tasks more often show oscillatory or increasing trajectories. These patterns motivate an empirical regime interpretation of decoding behavior. We propose \textbf{EDRM} (Entropy Dynamics-based Reasoning Manifold), a lightweight routing framework whose default heuristic requires no router training. EDRM summarizes early entropy trajectories with a compact, interpretable representation and selects among Direct, Standard, and CoT decoding. Across 15 benchmarks and 7 LLMs, its global and instance-level variants offer \textbf{16--55\%} token savings at comparable accuracy. The Full instance-level configuration combines routing with a short Direct fallback branch; our heuristic-router ablation reports up to \textbf{4.9 percentage points} of additional accuracy from oracle answer selection over routing without fallback. These results support selective reasoning based on early decoding signals and demonstrate the potential of entropy-based control for efficient LLM inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[Markovian Generation Chains in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11228v2)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.11228v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The widespread use of large language models (LLMs) raises an important question: how do texts evolve when they are repeatedly processed by LLMs? | **[PAIR: Preference-Aligned Intent Retrieval for Premium People Recommendation at LinkedIn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30941v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30941v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose embeddings trained on profile similarity often misrank candidates in professional networks, where relevance is inherently complementary rather than symmetric. However, this does not hold in professional platforms like LinkedIn, where interactions are often goal-driven and cross-role interactions, requiring matching based on complementary intents rather than similarity or social proximity. In this paper, we propose Preference-Aligned Intent Retrieval, a retrieval framework that is designed to model user intent preference instead of profile similarity. Our approach is inspired by the preference-learning perspective of Direct Preference Optimization, but instantiates preference learning as an exposure-conditioned multi-negative contrastive objective over embeddings. Furthermore, we incorporate user interaction history by aggregating click histories with positional encoding to capture temporal order, which enables constant-cost updates of user representations over a fixed history window, allowing new interactions to be incorporated without re-encoding history. As a result, personalization can be updated efficiently using lightweight CPU operations without repeated LLM inference during serving. We evaluate PAIR on LinkedIn's Premium retrieval task. | **[Low-Latency MoE Inference in Space Data Centers: A Wireless-Power-Transfer Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30923v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30923v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Space data centers (SpaceDCs) are emerging as platforms for large language models (LLMs), supported by continuous solar-energy harvesting and high-speed interconnections. Consequently, active experts remain power-constrained while harvested power at idle experts is underused. These reduce inference efficiency and increase latency. To address this supply-demand mismatch, we propose the SpaceWPT framework, which uses inter-satellite laser-based wireless power transfer (WPT) to redistribute harvested power. The key design challenge is to balance the computation demands of power-donating and power-receiving satellites under WPT losses: transferring more power accelerates computation at the receiver but leaves less power for the donor. After deriving design principles that simplify WPT control, we characterize the resulting latency tradeoff through a tractable surrogate of average inference latency. Minimizing the surrogate yields a novel two-sided water-filling strategy that determines whether each active expert donates, retains, or receives power according to the water level and its WPT link efficiency. | **[Amplifying Latency and Cost of LLM Inference via Rowhammer Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30798v1)** | 2026-10-22 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.30798v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) achieve high inference efficiency using optimized GPU kernels generated by just-in-time (JIT) compilation frameworks such as Triton. | **[PAGE404: Breaking GPU Isolation Through Hardware-assisted Page Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20962v3)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20962v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>NVIDIA's Grace Hopper (GH200) superchip represents a new generation of heterogeneous computing, tightly integrating a 72-core Arm-based CPU with a Hopper GPU on a single module via a high-bandwidth, cache-coherent interconnect. We further show that both channels are exploitable across Multi-Instance GPU (MIG) partitions, a feature intended to provide strong, hardware-enforced isolation between co-located tenants. Using proof-of-concept attacks, we build covert channels that reach roughly 8,500 bits per second through eviction-based signaling and 8,100 bits per second through migration-delay-based signaling across MIG instances. | **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29873v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. | **[RISK-KV: Quality-Aware Selective KV Cache Reconstruction for Distributed LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29544v1)** | 2026-10-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.29544v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Request migration and interruption recovery in distributed large language model (LLM) inference rely on historical key-value (KV) caches. | **[Performance-Driven Policy Optimization for Speculative Decoding with Adaptive Windowing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14978v3)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.14978v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by having a lightweight draft model propose speculative windows of candidate tokens for parallel verification by a larger target model. | **[How Developers Configure LLM Inference: A Large-Scale Study of Real-World Practices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27945v1)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.27945v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs are increasingly integrated into software systems, making deployment and inference configuration a recurring software engineering task. | **[Service-Induced Congestion in Memory-Constrained LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15555v3)** | 2026-10-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.15555v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language model (LLM) serving, each request accumulates persistent graphics processing unit (GPU) memory during service as its key-value cache grows with every generated token. | **[Magneton: Optimizing Energy Efficiency of ML Systems via Differential Energy Debugging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08365v2)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2512.08365v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The training and deployment of machine learning (ML) models have become extremely energy-intensive. | **[CEMA-Router: Infrastructure-Aware Batch Routing for LLM Inference in Cloud-Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26171v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26171v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLM) are increasingly integrated into pervasive applications spanning heterogeneous cloud-edge resources. | **[MX-Like Tensor Core for LLM Inference with NPU Integration via Hardware-Software Co-Design Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26083v1)** | 2026-10-19 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.26083v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Block-scaled quantization formats such as OCP Microscaling (MX) and NVFP4 have been widely adopted as efficient data formats for LLM inference. | **[ATEST: Adaptive Resource-Aware Test-Time Scaling for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25519v1)** | 2026-10-18 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.25519v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by sampling multiple answers, but fixed sampling budgets ignore both query difficulty and the changing energy cost of inference under GPU contention. | **[Hierarchical and Query-Aware Visual Token Pruning for High-Resolution LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24102v1)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.24102v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-resolution Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) preserve critical, fine-grained visual details by encoding a global thumbnail alongside multiple local crops. However, the resulting thousands of vision tokens introduce severe computational bottlenecks during Large Language Model (LLM) inference. Existing token pruning methods rely primarily on query-agnostic visual saliency, while current query-aware approaches either operate at a coarse crop-level resolution or compute token relevance too late, after vision tokens have already entered the heavy LLM layers. To address this, we propose a training-free, hierarchical token pruning framework that utilizes query guidance prior to executing any LLM blocks. Our top-down hierarchy first operates at the instance-level, dynamically scaling the vision token retention ratio of each sample based on its visual complexity. Next, at the crop-level, we distribute this sample budget across local crops using sentence-level cross-modal similarity. Finally, at the token-level, we exploit projected vision tokens alongside textual query token embeddings in the shared space to measure fine-grained alignment, applying a text-debiasing normalization to produce high-fidelity query scores. These query-guided budgets and scores are fused with local and global visual saliency metrics to select the most critical vision tokens. Extensive experiments on LLaVA-NeXT-7B demonstrate that our plug-and-play framework retains 98.0% and 94.8% of unpruned performance at 25% and 10% vision token retention, respectively. Notably, at 25% retention, our method delivers a 2.0x inference throughput speedup and even surpasses the unpruned baseline on POPE by 0.7 points.</p></details> | 6 pages, 2027 ICCE |
| **[DLite: History-free Speculative Decoding for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23787v1)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.23787v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding has become a mainstream technique for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight draft model to generate candidate tokens that are subsequently verified by the target model. However, agentic workloads introduce long and continuously evolving contexts and frequent multi-turn interactions. Under such workloads, we observe that existing draft models suffer from up to an 80% degradation in acceptance length, while maintaining their historical KV cache introduces substantial system overhead. In this paper, we identify the growing historical context as a common source of both problems and introduce DLite, a history-free speculative decoding framework for agentic AI. DLite directly reuses contextual hidden states from the target model and performs local speculative prediction without explicitly maintaining the historical context, transforming an increasingly complex modeling problem into local simple prediction problem. A lightweight sequential head further restores intra-block causal dependencies with low overhead. To effectively train this heterogeneous history-free architecture, we introduce a tailored two-stage training strategy. Experiments on general-purpose, long-context and agentic workloads show that DLite achieves 2-6 acceptance length and outperforms the state-of-the-art speculative decoding method, DSpark, up to 70%.</p></details> | 14 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Evidence-Bound Gateway-Path Provenance for Third-Party LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22560v2)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.22560v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Third-party LLM gateways select providers, models, endpoints, and fallback paths while also authoring the records used to explain and bill those decisions. An attested mediation runtime intersects a signed service policy with the client's authorization, constructs provider-facing calls, and protects streamed responses. The contract distinguishes initial routes from fallback destinations and admits each transition only after an authorized failure. A signed close-out binds the execution trace, provider-reported usage, authorized pricing, and complete response. We formalize the protocol state machine and establish authorization preservation, execution and stream binding, charge integrity, and channel confidentiality relative to the public receipt metadata. The analysis also characterizes replay, runtime replacement, and the information disclosed by public payload commitments. Controlled software-path measurements show 0.442 ms paired p95 latency overhead at concurrency one, peak throughput of 10.65 thousand requests/s, and stream close-out below 0.4 ms p95. An AWS Nitro Enclaves prototype completed 30 live single-route streaming requests with attestation-bound session and signing keys. The design separates gateway administration from payload processing and supplies a verifiable account of each confirmed execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 4 figures. Expanded protocol model and security analysis of authorization, request/stream binding, charge integrity, metadata leakage, and runtime lifecycle; existing measurements retained</p></details> |
| **[Is Your NPU Ready for LLMs? Dissecting the Hidden Efficiency Bottlenecks in Mobile LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05475v2)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.05475v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) on mobile devices enhances privacy and reduces latency, but is severely constrained by hardware inefficiencies. We present the first comprehensive, cross-layer measurement study of mobile LLM inference, spanning five mainstream frameworks (e.g., llama.cpp and GENIE) and three hardware backends (CPU, GPU, and NPU). To enable this analysis, we develop PowerBench, a fine-grained profiling tool that measures LLM throughput and provides the first backend-specific energy attribution for mobile LLM inference, going beyond traditional device-level measurements. We further conduct hardware microbenchmarks to characterize the memory-system performance and compute capabilities of mobile CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs. Our study yields three critical insights: (1) Framework-induced performance gaps are substantially amplified on NPUs, reaching up to $10\times$ due to differences in offloading strategies, hardware-underutilizing operators, and activation quantization strategies. (2) We identify a distinct ``phase split,'' in which NPUs excel at compute-bound prefilling, while CPUs outperform all other backends in memory-bound decoding. This split is driven by NPUs' preference for large, fixed-shape workloads, which conflicts with the small-kernel, dynamic nature of decoding. (3) Backend-specific profiling uncovers substantial scheduling headroom missed by prior work. Suboptimal thread configurations, uncoordinated NPU sleep latencies, and CPU polling intervals result in unnecessary energy consumption. For example, tuning CPU frequency alone reduces energy consumption by up to $39.7\%$. Leveraging these findings, we estimate that an energy-oriented configuration can reduce energy consumption by up to $53.2\%$ on the NPU backend across three datasets, highlighting the substantial potential for further efficiency improvements in mobile LLM deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM SIGMETRICS 2027</p></details> |
| **[Prism Attention: Proposal-Refined Index Sharing Mechanism for Efficient LLMs Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22985v1)** | 2026-10-16 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22985v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-context decoding in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly bottlenecked by the memory and computation required to attend over an ever-growing Key-Value (KV) cache at each autoregressive step. A common remedy is to share sparse indices across layers by computing them only at designated anchor layers, but such static reuse ignores the layer-wise evolution of attention patterns, causing the reused indices to progressively deviate from the layer-specific optima and incur substantial attention loss. By revisiting cross-layer sparsity from the perspective of attention dynamics, we find that inter-layer salient token shift is highly localized: although the exact top-$κ$ indices differ across layers, the core high-attention tokens remain stable, with changes concentrated near the expanded boundary of previously selected regions. Motivated by this, we propose Prism Attention, a proposal-refined sparse attention framework. It replaces rigid cross-layer index reuse with a coarse-to-fine strategy, preserving layer-wise adaptability while retaining the efficiency benefits of cross-layer sharing. Extensive experiments show that Prism Attention achieves state-of-the-art performance among mainstream sparse attention methods on both reasoning and long-context benchmarks, while attaining a $3.4\times$ end-to-end decoding speedup over FlashAttention at 128K context length.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published as a main conference paper at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[MemLLM: End-to-end In-Storage Acceleration of On-device Retrieval-Augmented Generation using Precomputed KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22579v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22579v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) on edge devices enables personalized large language model (LLM) inference while preserving privacy. To mitigate this, we propose MemLLM, an end-to-end in-storage processing (ISP) architecture that executes the complete attention pipeline based on precomputed KV caches within the SSD. MemLLM utilizes per-channel accelerators to fully utilize the aggregated NAND channel bandwidth and accelerates all KV cache-related computations on the storage-side to minimize PCIe transfers. To efficiently handle the sparse updates required by RAG, MemLLM introduces a fused recomputation-query kernel paired with a hardware-based filter logic to halve the NAND read volume and eliminate write-back penalties. Furthermore, MemLLM's global SRAM-based stateful reduction engine enables a completely DRAM-less dataflow, thereby removing the internal memory bottlenecks and bounding the PCIe egress to an $O(1)$ footprint. | **[Efficient KV Cache Migration for Geo-Distributed LLM Inference in Collaborative Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22172v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22172v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown exceptional capabilities but rely heavily on centralized cloud GPU clusters, leading to high bandwidth costs, latency, and privacy issues.
